Existence of non-trivial non-concentrated compactly supported stationary solutions of the 2D Euler equation with finite energy
Javier Gómez-Serrano, Jaemin Park, Jia Shi

Abstract
In this paper, we prove the existence of locally non-radial solutions to the stationary 2D Euler equations with compact support but non-concentrated around one or several points. Our solutions are of patch type, have analytic boundary, finite energy and sign-changing vorticity and are new to the best of our knowledge. The proof relies on a new observation that finite energy, stationary solutions with simply-connected vorticity have compactly supported velocity, and an application of the Nash-Moser iteration procedure.



1
Introduction

Throughout this paper we will work with the two-dimensional incompressible Euler equations in vorticity form. The evolution of the vorticity
\omega
is given by

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\omega+u\cdot\nabla\omega=0\ &\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
u(\cdot,t)=-\nabla^{\perp}(-\Delta)^{-1}\omega(\cdot,t)&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
\omega(\cdot,0)=\omega_{0}&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
\nabla^{\perp}:=(-\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{1}})
. Note that we can express
u
as

u(\cdot,t)=\nabla^{\perp}(\omega(\cdot,t)*\mathcal{N}),

where
\mathcal{N}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln|x|
is the Newtonian potential in two dimensions.

In this paper we will be focusing on constructing non-radial stationary solutions to equations (
1.1
). We will work in the
patch
setting, where
\omega(\cdot,t)=\sum\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}(t)}
is an sum of indicator functions of bounded sets that move with the fluid, although some of our results translate into the smooth setting as well (where
\omega(\cdot,t)
is smooth and compactly-supported in
x
).
See Remark
1.5
. For well-posedness results for patch solutions, see the global well-posedness results
[
8
,
21
]
.

Stationary solutions of the Euler equations are an important building block since they might play a role in many different directions: for example understanding turbulence both in 2D
[
11
]
and in 3D realizing turbulent flows as a superposition of Beltrami flows (which are particular stationary solutions of 3D Euler whose curl is proportional to themselves)
[
31
,
38
,
76
]
. In the context of numerical simulation, steady solutions of the 2D Euler equations were used by Chorin
[
27
]
to perform numerical simulations of the 2D Navier-Stokes equations with small viscosity, approximating the NS solutions as a superposition of steady eddies of constant vorticity that solve the 2D Euler equations. In the context of convex integration
[
37
,
9
]
, Beltrami flows and Mikado flows are classes of stationary solutions of the Euler equations that have been used within an iteration scheme to generate fast oscillating perturbations in order to construct weak solutions.

In
[
51
]
we showed that any stationary solution (in both the patch and smooth settings) for which
\omega\geq 0
and was compactly supported had to be radial. In this paper we want to address the necessity of the hypothesis
\omega\geq 0
by answering (on the positive) the following question:

Question 1.1

Do there exist non-trivial stationary solutions for which
\omega
changes sign?

Our first main result immediately gives the answer to the above question.

Theorem 1.2

(Corollary
3.2
)

There exist non-radial, sign-changing vortex patch solutions with analytic boundary to the 2D Euler equation (
1.1
) whose kinetic energy is infinite, that is,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla^{\perp}\omega*\mathcal{N}|^{2}dx=\infty
.

Remark 1.3

In
[
51
, Theorem A]
, it was shown that any non-negative stationary vortex patch must be radially symmetric up to a translation. Theorem
3.1
implies that by allowing an arbitrarily small portion of negative vorticity, one can find a non-radial stationary vortex patch. More precisely, for any
\epsilon>0
, one can find a non-radial stationary vortex patch
\omega
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{-}(x)dx<\epsilon
while
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{+}(x)dx
is uniformly bounded from below, where
\omega^{-}(x):=-\omega(x)1_{\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\omega(x)<0\right\}}
and
\omega^{+}(x):=\omega(x)1_{\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\omega(x)>0\right\}}
.

Our second main theorem concerns the solutions with
finite
kinetic energy. For the radial vorticity
\omega
with zero-average,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega(x)dx=0
, its velocity vanishes outside the support of
\omega
. With this observation, one can easily produce “globally non-radial” solutions by placing multiple copies of such vorticity so that their supports do not overlap. See Figure
1
. However the flow on each connected component of the support of the velocity is still circular (around different points). From now on, we say that such a solution is locally radial. More precisely a solution to the 2D Euler equation (
1.1
) is locally radial if each connected component of
\omega
is radial up to a translation and
\int_{\mathcal{C}}\omega dx=0
for each connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\text{supp}(\omega)
. The next theorem states that there exist more non-trivial stationary solutions.

Figure 1:
One can choose
\Theta
so that the radial vorticity has zero average (left). Since the support of such velocity and vorticity coincide, one can place such solutions to produce non-radial solutions (right).

Theorem 1.4

(Corollary
4.5
, Theorem
4.27
)

There exist vortex patch solutions to the 2D Euler equation (
1.1
) that are not locally radial, with finite kinetic energy and analytic boundary. Furthermore, the solutions have compactly supported velocity.

Remark 1.5

In this paper, we construct patch-type solutions with compactly supported velocity. This is a consequence of the existence of stationary solutions with finite kinetic energy and our key Lemma
4.3
. We note that a smooth stationary solution with finite kinetic energy also has compactly supported velocity (see Remark
4.6
).

1.1
2D Euler rigidity and construction of stationary solutions

In this subsection we will summarize some of the history of stationary solutions, mostly focusing on the rigidity (only trivial solutions exist) vs flexibility (non-trivial solutions exist) dichotomy. The first result goes back to Fraenkel
[
40
, Chapter 4]
, who proved that if
D
is a stationary, simply connected patch, then
D
must be a disk. The main idea uses the fact that in this setting, the stream function
\psi=1_{D}*\mathcal{N}
solves a semilinear elliptic equation
\Delta\psi=g(\psi)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
g(\psi)=1_{\{\psi<C\}}
, and one can apply the moving plane method developed in
[
79
,
47
]
using the monotonicity of
g
to obtain the symmetry of
\psi
. However, this result does not cover the non simply-connected case due to the fact that
\psi
may take different values on the different parts of the boundary and thus one can not apply moving plane techniques. This was solved by the authors and Yao in
[
51
]
using a variational approach that does not require this condition, and generalized to the smooth case as long as the vorticity is non-negative. Fraenkel’s result (and method) was generalized to other classes of active scalar equations such as the generalized SQG (where the velocity in (
1.1
) is given by the perpendicular gradient of the convolution with
\frac{1}{|x|^{\alpha}}
as opposed to the Newtonian potential) by
Reichel
[
77
, Theorem 2]
, Lu–Zhu
[
69
]
and Han–Lu–Zhu
[
55
]
in the case of
\alpha\in[0,1)
and Choksi–Neumayer–Topaloglu
[
26
]
in the case
\alpha\in[0,\frac{5}{3})
. In
[
51
]
we closed the problem for the full range
\alpha\in[0,2)
.

In the last few years, there has been an emergence of results on rigidity conditions, namely under which hypotheses we can guarantee that the solution has some rigid features in order to ultimately characterize stationary solutions by other geometric properties (such as being a shear or being radial). These are usually referred as ‘Liouville” type of results. In the case of 2D Euler, Hamel–Nadirashvili in
[
54
,
52
]
proved that any stationary solution without a stagnation point must be a shear flow whenever the domain is a strip and also in
[
53
]
proved the corresponding rigidity (radial symmetry) result whenever the domain is a two-dimensional bounded annulus, an exterior circular domain, a punctured disk or a punctured plane. Constantin–Drivas–Ginsberg
[
29
,
28
]
obtained rigidity and flexibility results for Euler and other equations (such as MHD) in both 2D and 3D. Coti-Zelati–Elgindi–Widmayer
[
32
]
constructed stationary solutions close to the Kolmogorov and Poiseuille flows in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. In the case of 2D Navier–Stokes, Koch–Nadirashvili–Seregin–Šverák also proved a Liouville theorem in
[
66
]
. See also
[
49
,
50
]
where together with Yao we proved rigidity and flexibility results for the vortex sheet problem.

We now review some additional results related to the characterization or construction of nontrivial stationary solutions to 2D Euler (flexibility). Nadirashvili
[
74
]
, following Arnold
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
studied the geometry and the stability of stationary solutions. When the problem is posed on a surface, Izosimov–Khesin
[
63
]
characterized stationary solutions of 2D Euler. Choffrut–Šverák
[
22
]
showed that locally near each stationary smooth solution there exists a manifold of stationary smooth solutions transversal to the foliation, and Choffrut–Székelyhidi
[
23
]
showed that there is an abundant set of stationary weak (
L^{\infty}
) solutions near a smooth stationary one. Shvydkoy–Luo
[
70
,
71
]
looked at stationary smooth solutions of the form
v=\nabla^{\perp}(r^{\gamma}f(\omega))
, where
(r,\omega)
are polar coordinates and were able to obtain a classification of them. In a different direction, Turkington
[
82
]
used variational methods to construct stationary vortex patches of a prescribed area in a bounded domain, imposing that the patch is a characteristic function of the set
\{\Psi>0\}
, and also studied the asymptotic limit of the patches tending to point vortices. He also studied the case of unbounded domains. We emphasize that those solutions do not have finite energy, unless the domain is bounded. Long–Wang–Zeng
[
68
]
studied the regularity in the smooth setting (see also
[
14
]
) as well as their stability. For other variational constructions close to point vortices, we would like to mention the work done by Cao–Liu–Wei
[
12
]
, Cao–Peng–Yan
[
13
]
and Smets–van Schaftingen
[
81
]
. Musso–Pacard–Wei
[
73
]
constructed nonradial smooth stationary solutions with finite energy but without compact support in
\omega
.
Our solutions are different from all of these constructions since they are not close to point vortices.

The (nonlinear
L^{1}
) stability of circular patches was proved by Wan–Pulvirenti
[
83
]
and later Sideris–Vega gave a shorter proof
[
80
]
. See also Beichman–Denisov
[
6
]
for similar results on the strip. Recently, Choi–Lim
[
25
]
generalized the stability results for radial patches to radially symmetric monotone vorticity.
Lately, Gavrilov
[
45
,
46
]
managed to construct nontrivial stationary solutions of 3D Euler with compactly supported velocity, which was further simplified and extended to other equations by Constantin–La–Vicol
[
30
]
. In
[
39
]
, Domínguez-Vázquez–Enciso–Peralta-Salas construct a different family of non-localizable, stationary solutions of 3D Euler that are axisymmetric with swirl. Regarding the stability results in 3D, we refer to the work of Choi
[
24
]
for solutions near Hill’s spherical vortex.

1.2
Structure of the proofs

The skeleton of our proof employs bifurcation theory, trying to find a perturbation of trivial solutions (radial vorticity). A very natural procedure is to frame it using a Crandall-Rabinowitz Theorem approach (see
[
18
,
19
,
16
,
17
,
20
,
41
,
42
,
44
,
43
,
48
,
56
,
58
,
36
,
59
,
57
]
for applications in the context of fluid mechanics). In a suitable functional setting, the problem boils down to finding non-trivial zeros of a nonlinear functional of the form (see (
2.3
)) :

\mathcal{F}(\Theta,R)=0,\quad F:\mathbb{R}\times H^{k+1}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto H^{k}(\mathbb{T}),\quad k\geq 3,\text{ given that $\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0)=0$ for any $\Theta\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Note that the loss of derivatives attributes to the fact that each component of the functional in (
2.3
) takes account of the tangent vector of the boundaries.
An easy observation is that the non-degeneracy of the velocity at the trivial solution (more precisely, the angular velocity
\neq 0
) guarantees that the linearized functional is Fredholm. For a radial vorticity with infinite energy, this can be more explicitly revealed in the linearized operators obtained in Proposition
3.6
, where the the angular velocity contributes to the diagonal elements of the matrix
M_{n}
, which can also be seen in (
4.5
). However, for a radial vorticity whose average vanishes, the corresponding velocity field, which can be explicitly computed, vanishes outside the support of the vorticity. At such trivial solutions, the linearized functional fails to be Fredholm and one cannot directly apply the Crandall-Rabinowitz Theorem. Indeed, the degeneracy of the velocity on the outmost boundary yields a mismatch of the image spaces of the functional at the nonlinear level and the linear level in terms of the regularity. One possible attempt to overcome this issue is to find two bifurcation curves emanating from negative-average vorticity and positive-average vorticity that are close to each other and show that the two curves merge together forming a loop, using the strategy in
[
60
]
(see Figure
2
). It is not difficult to find (in the three-layer setting) values for
\Theta^{+}
and
\Theta^{-}
and doing numerical continuation one can observe those loops.

Figure 2:
One possible strategy: Find two bifurcation curves emanating from negative average and positive average and show that those two curves are connected. If it is possible, then there must be a solution with zero-average
(\Theta^{*},R^{*})
by continuity.

Our key Lemma
4.3
shows that the degeneracy of the velocity is not a special case at the trivial solutions, but a generic phenomenon. More precisely, if the vorticity
\omega
is stationary (not necessarily radial) with zero-average, then
u=0
outside the support of
\omega
as long as
\text{supp}(\omega)
is simply connected. This gives two crucial implications:

•

A non-trivial stationary solution near a trivial one cannot be obtained by using the implicit function theorem. In Figure
2
,
D_{R}\mathcal{F}(\Theta^{*},R^{*})
, the linearized operator at
(\Theta^{*},R^{*})
(if it exists) cannot be an isomorphism.

•

The mismatch in regularity between the image spaces described above may be treated in the sprit of Nash-Moser scheme with the use of an
approximate inverse
.

The first implication shows that one cannot use the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction which is a crucial tool in the strategy in
[
60
]
to find a loop of bifurcation curves and even more importantly in the original proof of the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem. Regarding the second implication, the Nash-Moser scheme has been well-adapted in the context of steady-state solutions
[
22
,
61
,
62
]
, dynamical solutions
[
67
,
78
]
and more recently, embedded into a KAM scheme in the context of quasiperiodic solutions for the water waves problem (see
[
5
]
and the references therein). In Section
4
, we will find a bifurcation curve without the use of such a reduction technique, and combine it with the Nash-Moser scheme in the following way:

1)

In Subsection
4.1
, we slightly modify our functional setting given in Section
2
so that
(\Theta,R)
represents a zero-average vorticity. We are led to find non-trivial zeros of the modified functional (see (
4.5
) for the definition of
G
)

G:\mathbb{R}\times H^{k+1}\mapsto H^{k},\quad\text{given that ${G}(\Theta,0)=0$ for any $\Theta\in\mathbb{R}$ }.

See also (
4.161
), where it is shown that each component of
G
is the tangential derivative of the stream function on each boundary component.

2)

We analyze the linearized operator
D_{R}G
to find
\Theta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0)
satisfies (Subsection
4.5.4
)

\bullet

Ker
(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))
is one-dimensional, that is, Ker
(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))=\text{span}\left\{v\right\}
for some
v\in C^{\infty}
.

\bullet

Im
(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))^{\perp}
is one-dimensional.

\bullet

(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))
satisfies the transversality condition:
\partial_{\Theta}D_{R}(G(\Theta^{*},0))[v]\notin\text{Im}D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0)
.

This indicates that a possible non-trivial solution can be found as a zero of the following functional for sufficiently small
s>0
:

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{s}:H^{k+1}\mapsto H^{k},\quad\tilde{G}_{s}(R):=G(\Theta^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)[R]),

(1.2)

where
P:H^{k+1}\mapsto C^{\infty}
is the projection to
\text{Ker}(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))
, and
I
is the identity operator (see (
4.19
)).

3)

We perform Newton’s method for the functional
\tilde{G}_{s}
. The two important ingredients for Newton’s method to work are 1) a sufficiently good initial guess and 2) the invertibility of the linearized operator
D\tilde{G}_{s}
. For the initial guess, we already have
\tilde{G}_{s}=O(s^{2})
, since
v\in\text{Ker}(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))
.

4)

Regarding the second ingredient, we observe that the operator
D_{R}G(\Theta,R)
can be decomposed

D_{R}G=a+A\text{ as in \eqref{decomp_11},}

where the operator
a(\Theta,R)
vanishes if
(\Theta,R)
corresponds to a stationary solution. This is due to the fact that the velocity on the outmost boundary vanishes by Lemma
4.3
. This observation leads us to decompose
D\tilde{G}_{s}
, which can be immediately computed from (
1.2
) as

D\tilde{G}_{s}=\partial_{\Theta}G\circ P+D_{R}G\circ(I-P)=\underbrace{\partial_{\Theta}G\circ P+A\circ(I-P)}_{=:T_{s}}+a\circ(I-P),\quad\text{ see \eqref{T_sdef}}.

Thanks to the transversality condition, the one dimensionality of
\text{Im}(D_{R}G(\Theta^{*},0))^{\perp}(=\text{Im}A(\Theta^{*},0)^{\perp}
since
a(\Theta^{*},0)=0
) is compensated by
\partial_{\Theta}G\circ P
. Indeed, it is the main goal of Subsection
4.3.1
to show that
T_{s}
is invertible.

5)

As described in 4) we do not have the invertibility of
D\tilde{G}_{s}
, however, the invertibility of
T_{s}
is enough for the iteration in (
4.58
) to work since
a
tends to
0
along the iteration steps towards the solution. More precisely,
T_{s}
plays a role of the approximate inverse of
D\tilde{G}_{s}
. The loss of derivatives occurring at each iteration step is treated by the use of regularizing operator in (
4.57
) in the spirit of the Nash-Moser scheme.

We note that our finite energy solutions consist of three-layered vortex patches, unlike the infinite energy case (See Subsection
3.1
and
4.1
). From the technical point of view, the two-layered, zero-average trivial solutions do not give a linearized operator that has finite dimensional Kernel. More precisely, one cannot find a pair of parameters
(b,\Theta)
such that 1) the matrix
M_{n}
in Proposition
3.6
has zero-determinant and 2) the corresponding
\omega
determined by
(b,\Theta)
has zero-average (see Remark
3.12
). An important aspect of such two-layered stationary vorticity is that its stream function
\psi
always satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\psi=g(\psi),\text{ for some $g:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}$}.

(1.3)

We emphasize that the solutions that we construct in this paper
cannot
be captured by (
1.3
) unlike the earlier works, for example,
[
81
,
32
,
28
]
. That is, there is no
g
such that the stream function
\psi
solves (
1.3
). This feature can been seen from the fact that the trivial solutions from which we bifurcate to obtain finite energy solutions exhibit non-monotone stream functions in the radial direction.

Lastly, we point out that the desingularization from point vortices is not applicable to find a stationary solution with finite energy, since a steady configuration of point vortices has zero vortex angular momentum, which together with the finite energy hypothesis implies that the individual circulations have to be all zero (see
[
75
, Lemma 1.2.1]
).

1.3
Organization of the paper

The paper is organized in the following way: Section
2
sets up the functional framework and the equation that a stationary solution has to satisfy. Section
3
proves Theorem
1.2
, in the easier case where the finite energy hypothesis is dropped. Finally, section
4
proves Theorem
1.4
in the full generality setting using the Nash-Moser scheme. The Appendix contains some technical background results used throughout the proof, as well as basic bifurcation theory and some integrals needed for the spectral study.

2
Functional equations and functional setting for stationary vortex patches

Let us consider vorticity
\omega
of the form
\omega:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\Theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
D_{i}
is a simply-connected domain for each
i=1,\ldots,n
. Suppose the boundary of
D_{i}
is parametrized by a time-dependent
2\pi
-periodic curve
z_{i}(\cdot,t):\mathbb{T}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The evolution equation of the boundary can be written as a system of equations,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z_{j}(x,t)\cdot\partial_{x}z_{j}(x,t)^{\perp}

\displaystyle=u(z_{j}(x,t),t)\cdot\partial_{x}z_{j}(x,t)^{\perp}.

(2.1)

where
u(\cdot,t):=\nabla^{\perp}\left(\omega(\cdot,t)*\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\cdot|\right)
is the velocity vector. We will look for stationary solutions to
(
2.1
)
. Let us assume that each
z_{i}
can be written as

\displaystyle z_{i}(x)=(b_{i}+R_{i}(x))(\cos(x),\sin(x)),\quad x\in\mathbb{T},

(2.2)

where
b_{i}
is a positive constant for each
i
. By plugging these parametrizations into (
2.1
), we are led to solve the following system for
b:=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
,
\Theta:=(\Theta_{1},\ldots,\Theta_{n})
and
R:=(R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})
:

\displaystyle 0=\mathcal{F}_{j}(b,\Theta,R):=u(z_{j}(\cdot))\cdot z_{j}^{\prime\perp}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{4\pi}\Theta_{i}u_{i,j}(R)\cdot z_{j}^{\prime\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{4\pi}\Theta_{i}\underbrace{\left(u_{i,j}^{\theta}(R)R_{j}^{\prime}-u_{i,j}^{r}(R)(b_{j}+R_{j})\right)}_{=:S_{ij}(b_{i},b_{j},R_{i},R_{j})}.

(2.3)

where
u_{i,j}(R)(x):=\nabla^{\perp}\left(1_{D_{i}}*\log|\cdot|^{2}\right)(z_{j}(x))
, which can be thought of as a contribution of the
i
th patch on the
j
th curve, and
u_{i,j}^{\theta}
and
u_{i,j}^{r}
are the angular and the radial components of
u_{i,j}
. More explicitly, we have

\displaystyle S_{ij}(b_{i},b_{j},R_{i},R_{j})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos(x-y)((b_{i}+R_{i}(y))R_{j}^{\prime}(x)-(b_{j}+R_{j}(x))R_{i}^{\prime}(y))

\displaystyle\times\log((b_{j}+R_{j}(x))^{2}+(b_{i}+R_{i}(y))^{2}-2(b_{j}+R_{j}(x))(b_{i}+R_{i}(y))\cos(x-y))dy

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin(x-y)((b_{i}+R_{i}(y))(b_{j}+R_{j}(x))+R_{i}^{\prime}(y)R_{j}^{\prime}(x))

\displaystyle\times\log((b_{j}+R_{j}(x))^{2}+(b_{i}+R_{i}(y))^{2}-2(b_{j}+R_{j}(x))(b_{i}+R_{i}(y))\cos(x-y))dy.

(2.4)

It is clear that
S_{ij}(b_{i},b_{j},0,0)=0,
since
u_{i,j}^{r}=0
for radial patches. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(b,\Theta,0)=0,\text{ for any $b,\Theta$.}

(2.5)

In what follows, we will pick one of
\Theta_{i}
as a bifurcation parameter, while the others are fixed.

We now proceed to discuss the functional spaces that we will use. In Section
3
we will work with the following analytic spaces. Following
[
17
]
, we denote the space of analytic functions in the strip
\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\text{Im}(z)\leq c\right\}
by
\mathcal{C}_{w}(c)
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we will consider the following spaces of
\frac{2\pi}{m}
-periodic functions:

\displaystyle X^{k,m}_{c}:=\left\{f(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{w}(c),\quad f(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{jm}\cos(jmx),\quad\rVert f\rVert_{X^{k,m}_{c}}<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle Y^{k,m}_{c}:=\left\{f(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{w}(c),\quad f(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{jm}\sin(jmx),\quad\rVert f\rVert_{Y^{k,m}_{c}}<\infty\right\},

where
\rVert f\rVert_{X^{k,m}_{c}}=\rVert f\rVert_{Y^{k,m}_{c}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|a_{jm}|^{2}(1+jm)^{2k}(\cosh(cjm)^{2}+\sinh(cjm)^{2})
.

In Section
4
we will consider the following spaces of
\frac{2\pi}{m}
-periodic functions:

\displaystyle X^{k,m}:=\left\{f(x)\in H^{k}(\mathbb{T}):\quad f(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{jm}\cos(jmx),\quad\rVert f\rVert_{X^{k,m}}<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle Y^{k,m}:=\left\{f(x)\in H^{k}(\mathbb{T}):\quad f(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{jm}\sin(jmx),\quad\rVert f\rVert_{Y^{k,m}}<\infty\right\},

where
\rVert f\rVert_{X^{k,m}}=\rVert f\rVert_{Y^{k,m}}:=\rVert f\rVert_{H^{k}(\mathbb{T})}
.

3
Warm-up: Existence of non-radial stationary vortex patches with infinite energy

As a warm-up, in this section we aim to show that there exists a non-trivial patch solution with infinite kinetic energy,
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\left(\omega*\log|\cdot|\right)|^{2}dx=\infty
. Recall that (
[
72
, Proposition 3.3]
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\left(\omega*\log|\cdot|\right)|^{2}dx<\infty\iff\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x)dx=0.

(3.1)

In our proof, we will find a continuous bifurcation curve, emanating from a two-layered vortex patch whose vorticity does not has zero mean.

3.1
Main results for infinite energy

Let us consider two-layer vortex patches, that is,
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
in the setting in Section
2
. For
b,\Theta\in(0,1)
, using the scaling invariance of the equations, we will choose the parameters to be

\displaystyle b_{1}=1,\quad b_{2}=b,\quad\Theta_{1}=\Theta,\quad\Theta_{2}=-1,

(3.2)

so that if
R_{1}=R_{2}=0
, then

\displaystyle\omega=\begin{cases}\Theta&\text{ in }B_{1}\backslash B_{b}\\
\Theta-1&\text{ in }B_{b},\end{cases}

(3.3)

where
B_{r}
denotes the unit disk centered at the origin.
Note that the case
\Theta=1
corresponds to an annulus. Later, we will fix
b
as well, and let
\Theta
play the role as a bifurcation parameter. With this setting, the system (
2.3
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle 0=\mathcal{F}(\Theta,R)=(F^{1}(\Theta,R),F^{2}(\Theta,R)),\quad R=(R_{1},R_{2}),

(3.4)

where we omit the dependence of
\Theta_{2}
and
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
for notational simplicity.

Theorem 3.1

Let
k,m
be such that
k\geq 3
,
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
b
satisfy the condition in Lemma
3.8
. Then for some
c>0
and
s_{0}=s_{0}(k,m,c,b)>0
, there exist two bifurcation curves
[0,s_{0})\ni s\mapsto(\Theta^{\pm}(s),R^{\pm}(s))\in\mathbb{R}\times(X^{k,m}_{c})^{2}
such that for each
s\in(0,s_{0})
,
(\Theta^{\pm}(s),R^{\pm}(s))
is a solution of the equation (
3.4
) and
R^{\pm}(s)\neq 0\in(X^{k,m}_{c})^{2}
. The bifurcation curve emanates from
(\Theta^{\pm}(0),R^{\pm}(0))=(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)
, where
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
are defined in Lemma
3.8
.

Theorem
3.1
immediately implies the existence of non-radial stationary vortex patches with infinite energy.

Corollary 3.2

Let
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is an
m
-fold symmetric stationary patch solution for the 2D Euler equation with analytic boundary and infinite kinetic energy, that is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\left(\omega*\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\cdot|\right)|^{2}dx=\infty.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1
, there are two continuous bifurcation curves
\Psi^{\pm}:[0,s_{0})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\times\left(X^{k,m}_{c}\right)^{2}
of solutions of (
3.4
) such that

\displaystyle\Psi^{\pm}(s)=\left(\Theta^{\pm}(s),R(s)\right),\quad F(\Psi^{\pm}(s))=0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\Psi^{\pm}(0)=\left(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0\right),

for some
s_{0}>0
. From (
2.1
) and (
2.3
), it is clear that for each
s\in(0,s_{0})
and for each choice of
\pm
,

\displaystyle\omega^{s}(x):=\begin{cases}\Theta-1&\text{ if }x\in D_{2}(s),\\
\Theta&\text{ if }x\in D_{1}(s)\backslash\overline{D_{2}(s)}\\
0&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

(3.5)

is a stationary solution to the Euler equation, where
D_{1}(s)
and
D_{2}(s)
are the bounded domains determined by (
2.2
) with
R(s)
. Since
R(s)\in\left(X^{k,m}_{c}\right)^{2}
, the boundaries are analytic.

Now we consider the kinetic energy of the solution. From (
3.1
), it suffices to show that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{0}(x)dx\neq 0
. By the continuity of the bifurcation curve, this immediately implies that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{s}(x)dx\neq 0
for small
s>0
. Then it follows from Lemma
3.11
that
b^{2}<\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
, hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega^{0}(x)dx=\pi\left(-b^{2}+\Theta_{m}^{\pm}\right)>0.

This completes the proof.
∎

The rest of this section will be devoted to prove Theorem
3.1
. The proof will be divided into 5 steps. These steps correspond to check the hypotheses of the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem
A.1
for our functional
F
in (
3.4
). The hypotheses in Theorem
A.1
can be read as follows in our setting:

1.

The functional
\mathcal{F}
satisfies

\mathcal{F}(\Theta,R)\,:\,(0,1)\times\{V^{\epsilon}\}\mapsto(Y^{k-1,m}_{c})^{2},

where
V^{\epsilon}
is an open neighborhood of 0,

\displaystyle V^{\epsilon}=\left\{(f,g)\in(X^{k,m}_{c})^{2}\,:\,||f||_{X^{k,m}_{c}}+||g||_{X^{k,m}_{c}}<\epsilon\right\}

for some
\epsilon>0
and
k\geq 3
.

2.

\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0)=0
for every
0<\Theta<1
.

3.

The partial derivatives
\partial_{\Theta}\mathcal{F}
,
D\mathcal{F}
,
\partial_{\Theta}D\mathcal{F}
exist and are continuous, where
D\mathcal{F}
is Gateaux derivative of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to the functional variable
R
.

4.

Ker
(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0))\subset(X^{k,m}_{c})^{2}
and
(Y^{k-1,m}_{c})^{2}
/Im(
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)
) are one-dimensional (see Proposition
3.8
for the definition of
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
).

5.

\partial_{\Theta}D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)[v_{0}]\not\in
Im(
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)
), where
v_{0}
is a non-zero element in Ker
(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0))
.

Remark 3.3

We remark that if
i\neq j
then the functions inside the logarithm in
S_{i,j}
in (
2
) are uniformly bounded from below in
y
for all
x
by a strictly positive constant depending on the parameters. Then we can analytically extend the integrand in
x
to the strip
|\Im(z)|\leq c
in such a way that the real part of this extension stays uniformly bounded away from 0 for a small enough
c
. The case
i=j
can be treated similarily as in
[
17
, Remark 2.1]
.

3.2
Proof of Theorem
3.1

3.2.1
Steps 1,2 and 3: Regularity

In order to check the first three steps, it suffices to check if
S_{ij}
in (
2
) satisfies the hypotheses, since
F
is a linear combination of
S_{ij}
. As mentioned in Remark
3.3
, the case
i\neq j
is trivial since there is no singularity in the integrand and analytically extended into a strip in
\mathbb{C}
, if
(R_{1},R_{2})
is in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
(0,0)
. For
i=j
, the first three steps with slightly different settings were already done in the literature. For example, step 1 can be done in the same way as in
[
35
]
. Step 2 follows immediately from (
2.5
). Existence and continuity of the Gateaux derivatives for the gSQG equation was done in
[
48
]
and the same proof can be adapted to our setting straightforwardly.

3.2.2
Step 4: Analysis of the linear part.

In this section, we will focus on the spectral study of the Gateaux derivative
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0):=D_{R}\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0)
.

Calculation of
D\mathcal{F}

We aim to express the Gateaux derivative of
\mathcal{F}
around
(\Theta,0)
in the direction
(H(x),h(x))
in terms of Fourier series.

Lemma 3.4

Let
S_{ij}
be defined as in (
2
). Then:

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}S_{ij}(b_{i},b_{j},th_{i},th_{j})\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\int b_{i}(h_{j}^{\prime}(x)\cos(x-y)-h_{i}^{\prime}(y))\log(b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y))dy=:\mathcal{L}_{1}+\mathcal{L}_{2}.

Proof.

Let
V^{1,ab}_{ij}
(resp.
V^{2,ab}_{ij}
) be the contribution of the first term (resp. second term) of (
2
) where the first factor contributes with a
t^{a}
and the second with
t^{b}
. We are looking for all combinations such that
a+b=1
. We start looking at the first summand. We have that:

\displaystyle V_{ij}^{1,10}

\displaystyle=\int\cos(x-y)(b_{i}h_{j}^{\prime}(x)-b_{j}h_{i}^{\prime}(y))\log(b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y))dy.

Similarly, for the second one,

\displaystyle V_{ij}^{2,01}

\displaystyle=-2\int\sin(x-y)(b_{i}b_{j})\frac{h_{j}(x)(b_{j}-b_{i}\cos(x-y))+h_{i}(y)(b_{i}-b_{j}\cos(x-y))}{b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y)}dy,

\displaystyle V_{ij}^{2,10}

\displaystyle=-\int\sin(x-y)(b_{i}h_{j}(x)+b_{j}h_{i}(y))\log(b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y))dy,

where we have used Lemma
A.2
. Integrating by parts in
V_{ij}^{2,01}
:

\displaystyle V_{ij}^{2,01}

\displaystyle=\int((b_{i}h_{j}(x)+b_{j}h_{i}(y))\sin(x-y)-h_{i}^{\prime}(y)(b_{i}-b_{j}\cos(x-y)))\log(b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y))dy,

Finally, adding all the log terms and the non-log terms together:

\displaystyle V_{ij}^{1,10}+V_{ij}^{2,01}+V_{ij}^{2,10}

\displaystyle=\int b_{i}(h_{j}^{\prime}(x)\cos(x-y)-h_{i}^{\prime}(y))\log(b_{j}^{2}+b_{i}^{2}-2b_{j}b_{i}\cos(x-y))dy,

as we wanted to prove.
∎

Lemma 3.5

Let
h_{i}=A_{i}\cos(mx)
and
r=\frac{\min\{b_{i},b_{j}\}}{\max\{b_{i},b_{j}\}}
. Then:

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}S_{ij}(b_{i},b_{j},th_{i},th_{j})\right|_{t=0}=2\pi\sin(mx)mb_{i}\left(A_{j}r-A_{i}\frac{r^{m}}{m}\right).

Proof.

From Lemma
3.4
and Corollary
A.4
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}

\displaystyle=-mb_{i}A_{j}\sin(mx)\mathcal{A}_{0}(r,1),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}

\displaystyle=mb_{i}A_{i}\sin(mx)\mathcal{A}_{0}(r,m),

Adding the two contributions gives the desired result.
∎

Note that the functional
\mathcal{F}
is a linear combination of
S_{ij}
(see (
2.3
)). Using the above two lemmas, we obtain the following proposition:

Proposition 3.6

Let
h(x)=\sum_{n}a_{n}\cos(nx),

H(x)=\sum_{n}A_{n}\cos(nx)
, then we have that:

\displaystyle D\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0)[H,h]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U(x)\\
u(x)\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle U(x)=\sum_{n}U_{n}\sin(nx),\quad u(x)=\sum_{n}u_{n}\sin(nx),

and the coefficients satisfy, for any
n
:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U_{n}\\
u_{n}\end{array}\right):=(-n)M_{n}(\Theta)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{n}\\
a_{n}\end{array}\right):=(-n)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{b^{2}}{2}-\frac{\Theta}{2}+\frac{\Theta}{2n}&-\frac{b^{n+1}}{2n}\\
\Theta\frac{b^{n}}{2n}&-\frac{b}{2n}+\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta)\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{n}\\
a_{n}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

It follows from (
3.2
), the definition of
\mathcal{F}
in (
3.4
), (
2.3
) and Lemma
3.5
.
∎

One dimensionality of the Kernel of the linear operator.

Our goal here is to verify the one dimensionality of Ker(
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta,0)
) for some
\Theta
. More precisely, we will prove the following proposition:

Proposition 3.7

Fix
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
and take any
b\in(0,b_{m})
, where
b_{m}
is as in Lemma
3.8
. Then there exist two
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}\in(0,1)
, such that Ker
(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0))
is one-dimensional. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\text{Ker}(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0,0))=\text{span}\left\{v_{0}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\cos(mx):=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2m}b-\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\\
\frac{\Theta_{m}^{\pm}}{2m}b^{m}\end{pmatrix}\cos(mx)\right\}\subset X^{k,m}_{c}\times X^{k,m}_{c}.

The proof of the above proposition relies on the analysis of the matrix
M_{n}
in Lemma
3.8
and
3.9
, which we will prove below.

Lemma 3.8

Let
\Delta_{m}(\Theta)
be

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(\Theta):=\frac{4m^{2}}{b}\text{det}(M_{m}(\Theta))=\left(\Theta b^{2m}+b^{2}m(m(1-\Theta)-1)+\Theta(1-m)(m(1-\Theta)-1)\right)

(3.6)

Then, for any
m\geq 2
, there exists
0<b_{m}<1
such that for any
0<b<b_{m}<1
, there exists
0<\Theta^{-}_{m}<\Theta^{+}_{m}<1
such that
\Delta_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=0
. We also have that rk
(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))=1
for those values of
\Theta^{+}_{m}
,
\Theta^{-}_{m}
, where
rk(A)
is the rank of a matrix
A
.

Proof.

For fixed
m
, we study the polynomial
\Delta_{m}(\Theta)
.
We need to solve

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(\Theta)=m(m-1)\Theta^{2}-((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})\Theta+b^{2}m(m-1)=0.

(3.7)

Since
\Delta_{m}(\Theta)
is a quadratic function, we only need to show that the discriminant is positive. We have

\displaystyle D_{m}:=((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})^{2}-4m^{2}(m-1)^{2}b^{2}

\displaystyle=((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m}-2m(m-1)b)((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m}+2m(m-1)b)

\displaystyle=(m-1-bm-b^{m})(m-1-bm+b^{m})((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m}+2m(m-1)b)

\displaystyle=:D_{m,1}\cdot D_{m,2}\cdot D_{m,3}.

Since
m\geq 2
,
0<b<1
, we have
D_{m,3}>1-1=0
. We also have

D_{m,2}=m(1-b)+b^{m}-1=(1-b)[m-\frac{1-b^{m}}{1-b}]=(1-b)(m-(1+b+b^{2}+...+b^{m-1}))>0.

D_{m,1}
is decreasing in
b
when
b\geq 0
and
D_{m,1}(0)=m-1>0
,
D_{m,1}(1)=-2
. Let
b_{m}
be the only zero point of
D_{m,1}
in (0,1). If we take
0<b<b_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle D_{1,m}>0,D_{m}>0.

(3.8)

Hence
\Delta_{m}(\Theta)
has two different solutions
\Theta_{m}^{-}<\Theta_{m}^{+}
.
Moreover, the matrix does not vanish when
b\neq 0
, implying rk
(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))=1
.
Now we are left to show
0<\Theta_{m}^{\pm}<1
. We have

\Theta_{m}^{+}\Theta_{m}^{+}=b^{2}<1,

and

\Theta_{m}^{+}+\Theta_{m}^{-}=\frac{((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})}{m(m-1)}\geq\frac{1-1}{m(m-1)}>0.

Hence,
0<\Theta_{m}^{-}<1
and
0<\Theta_{m}^{+}
. If
\Theta_{m}^{+}\geq 1
, then
\Delta_{m}(1)\leq 0
. However,

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(1)=b^{2m}-mb^{2}-(1-m)

(3.9)

\displaystyle=(1-b^{2})(m-\frac{1-b^{2m}}{1-b^{2}})

\displaystyle=(1-b^{2})(m-1-b^{2}-...-b^{2m-2})>0.

Therefore
0<\Theta_{m}^{\pm}<1
.
∎

We now show that
\Delta_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\neq 0
for any
j\neq 1
.

Lemma 3.9

Let
j>1
and let
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
and
\Theta_{jm}^{\pm}
be defined as in the previous Lemma. Then
\Theta_{jm}^{+}>\Theta_{m}^{+}>\Theta_{m}^{-}>\Theta_{jm}^{-}
. Hence,
M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
is non-singular for all
j>1
.

Proof.

Since
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
solves the equation

\Theta^{2}-\frac{((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})}{m(m-1)}\Theta+b^{2}=0.

We only need to show
F(b,m):=\frac{((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})}{m(m-1)}
is strictly increasing with respect to
m
.
We have

\displaystyle F(b,m+1)-F(b,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{(m^{2}+b^{2}(m+1)^{2}-b^{2(m+1)})}{(m+1)m}-\frac{((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})}{m(m-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(m^{2}+b^{2}(m+1)^{2}-b^{2(m+1)})(m-1)-((m-1)^{2}+b^{2}m^{2}-b^{2m})(m+1)}{(m+1)m(m-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{b^{2}((m+1)^{2}(m-1)-m^{2}(m+1))+m^{2}(m-1)-(m+1)(m-1)^{2}-b^{2m+2}(m-1)+(m+1)b^{2m}}{(m+1)m(m-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{-b^{2}(m+1)+m-1-b^{2m+2}(m-1)+b^{2m}(m+1)}{(m+1)m(m-1)}.

Thus

\displaystyle F(b,m+1)-F(b,m)>0\Leftrightarrow-b^{2}(m+1)+m-1-b^{2m+2}(m-1)+b^{2m}(m+1)>0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(1+b^{2})(-1+b^{2m})-(-1+b^{2})(1+b^{2m})m>0\Leftrightarrow-(1+b^{2})(b^{2m-2}+b^{2m-4}...+b^{2}+1)+(1+b^{2m})m>0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow-\sum_{k=1}^{m}b^{2m-2k}-\sum_{k=1}^{m}b^{2k}+(1+b^{2m})m>0\Leftrightarrow\sum_{k=1}^{m}(1-b^{2k})(1-b^{2m-k})>0.

It is easy to show the last inequality since
0<b<1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.7
:

Let
m\geq 2
and let
b
,
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
be as defined in Lemma
3.8
. Assume that
H(x)=\sum_{j}A_{jm}\cos(jmx)
and
h(x)=\sum_{j}a_{jm}\cos(jmx)
satisfy

DF(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)[H,h]=(0,0).

Then it follows from Proposition
3.6
that

\displaystyle M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\begin{pmatrix}A_{jm}\\
a_{jm}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

For all
j>1
, it follows from Lemma
3.9
that
M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
is invertible, thus
A_{jm}=a_{jm}=0
. For
j=1
, Lemma
3.8
tells us that
(A_{m},a_{m})\in\text{Ker}(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))=\text{span}\left\{v_{0}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}):=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2m}b-\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\\
\frac{\Theta_{m}^{\pm}}{2m}b^{m}\end{pmatrix}\right\}
. This finishes the proof.

\Box

Codimension of the image of the linear operator.

We now characterize the image of
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)
. We have the following proposition:

Proposition 3.10

Let

\displaystyle Z=\left\{(Q,q)\in Y^{k-1,m}_{c}\times Y^{k-1,m}_{c},Q(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}Q_{jm}\sin(jmx),q(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}q_{jm}\sin(jmx),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\exists\lambda_{Q,q}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ s.t.}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}Q_{m}\\
q_{m}\end{array}\right)=\lambda_{Q,q}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{1}{2m}b^{m+1}\\
-\frac{1}{2m}b+\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Then
Z=\text{Im}\left(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0,0)\right)
.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
3.6
,
\text{Im}\left(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)\right)\subset Z
is trivial, since
M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
is non-singular for
j>1
, and
\text{Im}(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))=\text{span}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2m}b^{m+1}\\
-\frac{1}{2m}b+\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta)\end{pmatrix}\right\}.
In order to prove
\text{Im}\left(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)\right)\supset Z
, we need to check whether the possible preimage satisfies the desired regularity. To do so, we have the following asymptotic lemma:

Lemma 3.11

For fixed
m\geq 2
and
b
defined as in Proposition
3.7
, we have
b^{2}-\Theta_{m}^{\pm}<0
and

\displaystyle\frac{b}{4j^{2}m^{2}}\Delta_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=\frac{b}{4}\left(b^{2}-\Theta_{m}^{\pm}\right)(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})+O\left(\frac{1}{jm}\right),\quad\text{ as }j\to\infty,

(3.10)

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\text{det}{(M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))^{-1}}\lesssim_{m,\Theta}1,\quad\text{ for sufficiently large }j.

(3.11)

Remark 3.12

As shown in the above lemma, there is no bifurcation curve from the two-layered vortex patch with zero-average. This is due to the fact that the radial vorticity
\omega
determined by
b
and
\Theta^{\pm}
as in (
3.3
) satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega dx=\pi\left(-b^{2}+\Theta_{m}^{\pm}\right)>0
. Note that if we require
\Theta=b^{2}
to ensure
\int\omega dx=0
, it follows from (
3.6
) that
\Delta_{m}(b^{2})=mb^{2}(1-b^{2})+b^{2}(b^{2m}-1)
, which does not vanish for any
m\geq 2
unless
b=0
or
b=1
. Therefore, for any
0<b<1
, the linearized operator is an isomorphism and the implicit function theorem shows that there cannot be a bifurcation.

Proof of Lemma
3.11
:

First we show that
b^{2}<\Theta_{m}^{\pm}
. By Lemma
3.8
,
\Theta_{m}^{+}\Theta_{m}^{-}=b^{2}
and
0<\Theta_{m}^{\pm}<1
. Thus
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}>b^{2}
. Therefore the first assertion is proved. The second assertion follows directly from (
3.6
) since

\displaystyle\frac{b}{4(jm)^{2}}\Delta_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=\frac{b}{4(jm)^{2}}\left((b^{2}-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})(jm)^{2}+O\left(m\right)\right)=\frac{b}{4}\left(b^{2}-\Theta_{m}^{\pm}\right)(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})+O\left(\frac{1}{jm}\right).

Lastly, by choosing
j
large so that
|\frac{b}{4(jm)^{2}}\Delta_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})|>\frac{b}{8}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}-b^{2})(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})>0
, we have

\displaystyle|\text{det}(M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})^{-1})|=\frac{1}{|\frac{b}{4(jm)^{2}}\Delta_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})|}\lesssim 1,

which proves (
3.11
).

\Box

Now for an element
(Q,q)\in Z
, let
(H,h)
be such that

\displaystyle H(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}A_{jm}\cos(jmx),\quad h(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{jm}\cos(jmx),

with

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A_{m}\\
a_{m}\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{1}{m}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\lambda_{Q,q}\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}A_{jm}\\
a_{jm}\end{pmatrix}=(-jm)^{-1}M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}Q_{jm}\\
q_{jm}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ for $j>1$}.

It is clear from (
3.6
) that
D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0,0)(H,h)=(Q,q).
We will prove that
(H,h)\in X^{k,m}_{c}\times X^{k,m}_{c}
.
From Lemma
3.11
and the fact that
M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
is nonsingular for
j>1
, it follows that

\displaystyle|A_{jm}|^{2}+|a_{jm}|^{2}\lesssim(jm)^{-2}\left(|Q_{jm}|^{2}+|q_{jm}|^{2}\right)\quad\text{ for all }j>1.

(3.12)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\rVert H\rVert_{X^{k,m}_{c}}^{2}+\rVert h\rVert_{X^{k,m}_{c}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(|A_{jm}|^{2}+|a_{jm}|^{2}\right)(1+jm)^{2k}(\cosh(cjm)^{2}+\sinh(cjm)^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{m^{2}}\lambda_{Q,q}^{2}(1+m)^{2k}(\cosh(cm)^{2}+\sinh(cm)^{2})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}(jm)^{-2}\left(|Q_{jm}|^{2}+|q_{jm}|^{2}\right)(1+jm)^{2k}(\cosh(cjm)^{2}+\sinh(cjm)^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+(\rVert Q\rVert_{Y^{k-1,m}_{c}}^{2}+\rVert q\rVert_{Y^{k-1,m}_{c}}^{2})

\displaystyle<\infty.

This proves that
(H,h)\in X^{k,m}_{c}\times X^{k,m}_{c}
, and therefore
Z\subset\text{Im}\left(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)\right)
.
∎

3.2.3
Step 5: Transversality

Proposition 3.13

We have that

\displaystyle\partial_{\Theta}D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)[v_{0}]\not\in\text{Im}(D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)),

(3.13)

where
v_{0}=v_{0}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
is as given in Proposition
3.7
.

Proof.

For
h(x)=\sum_{j}a_{jm}\cos(jmx),

H(x)=\sum_{j}A_{jm}\cos(jmx)
, we have that (see Proposition
3.6
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\Theta}D\mathcal{F}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm},0)[H,h]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U(x)\\
u(x)\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle U(x)=\sum_{j}U_{jm}\sin(jmx),\quad u(x)=\sum_{j}u_{jm}\sin(jmx),

and the coefficients satisfy, for any
j
:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U_{jm}\\
u_{jm}\end{array}\right):=(-jm)\partial_{\Theta}M_{jm}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{jm}\\
a_{jm}\end{array}\right):=(-jm)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2m}&0\\
\frac{b^{m}}{2m}&-\frac{b}{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{jm}\\
a_{jm}\end{array}\right).

Letting

\displaystyle v_{0}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{b}{2m}-\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\\
\frac{\Theta_{m}^{\pm}}{2m}b^{m}\end{array}\right),\quad w(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{1}{2m}b^{m+1}\\
-\frac{1}{2m}b+\frac{b}{2}(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\end{array}\right),

be the generators of Ker
(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))
and Im
(M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}))
respectively, the transversality condition is equivalent to prove that
w_{1}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
and
w(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})
are not parallel, where

\displaystyle w_{1}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\Theta}M_{m}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})v_{0}(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{b}{4}\left(\frac{1}{m}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{m}-(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\right)\\
\frac{b^{m+1}}{4m}\left(\frac{1}{m}-1\right)\end{array}\right)

This is equivalent to prove that:

\displaystyle 0\neq-\frac{b^{2}}{8}\left(\frac{1}{m}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{m}-(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})\right)^{2}+\frac{b^{2m+2}}{8m^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{m}-1\right)\Leftrightarrow\left(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}-(1-\frac{1+b^{m}}{m})\right)\left(\Theta_{m}^{\pm}-(1-\frac{1-b^{m}}{m})\right)\neq 0.

We prove it by contradiction. If
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1+b^{m}}{m}
, we have
b^{m}=m(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})-1
.
Moreover, by (
3.6
),we have

\Theta_{m}^{\pm}b^{2m}+b^{m+2}m+\Theta_{m}^{\pm}(1-m)b^{m}=0.

Hence,

\displaystyle\Theta_{m}^{\pm}b^{m}+b^{2}m+\Theta_{m}^{\pm}(1-m)=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\Theta_{m}^{\pm}(m(1-\Theta_{m}^{\pm})-1)+b^{2}m+\Theta_{m}^{\pm}(1-m)=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow-m(\Theta_{m}^{\pm})^{2}+b^{2}m=0.

Since
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}>0,b>0
, we have

\Theta_{m}^{\pm}=b,

implying a contradiction since
\Theta_{m}^{+}\Theta_{m}^{-}=b^{2}
and
\Theta_{m}^{+}\neq\Theta_{m}^{-}
. If
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}=1-\frac{1-b^{m}}{m}
, we can follow the same way to get
\Theta_{m}^{\pm}=b
and get a contradiction.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
:

All the hypotheses of the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem were checked in Propositions
3.7
,
3.10
and
3.13
. Therefore the desired result follows immediately.

\Box

4
Existence of non-radial stationary vortex patches with finite energy

In this section, we aim to prove that there exist non-trivial patch solutions with finite kinetic energy,
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\left(\omega*\log|\cdot|\right)|^{2}dx<\infty
. As mentioned in (
3.1
), this property is equivalent to
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega dx=0
. By Remark
3.12
, we can not use two-layer patches and instead we will consider three-layer patches.

4.1
Main results for finite energy

We consider vortex patches with three layers, that is,
i\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
in the setting in Section
2
. The total vorticity that we consider is of the form
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
, where
D_{i}
is determined by
\partial D_{i}=\left\{(b_{i}+R_{i}(x))(\cos x,\sin x):x\in\mathbb{T}\right\}
. We will look for a bifurcation curve from the radial one,
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Theta_{i}1_{B_{b_{i}}}
, where
B_{r}
denotes the disk with radius
r
centered at the origin. We have the following parameters and functional variables:

•

b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
: the radii of the different layers of the annuli. We will have
1=:b_{1}>b_{2}>b_{3}
.

•

\Theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
: the vorticity at the different layers. We will choose
\Theta_{1}:=1
.

•

R:=(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in(X^{k,m})^{3}
, for some
3\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
: the functional variables that determine the boundaries.

In the rest of this section, we will fix
m
,
b_{2}
and
\Theta_{2}
so that for
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle 0<b_{2}<\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2m}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{m({b_{2}}^{2}-1)}{(1-{b_{2}}^{2m})b_{2}^{2}}<\Theta_{2}<\min\bigg\{2\frac{{b_{2}}^{2m-2}(b_{2}^{2}-1)m}{1-b_{2}^{2m}},\ \frac{-1}{b_{2}^{2}}\bigg\}.

(4.1)

Given
R
,
\Theta_{i}
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, we choose
b_{3}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x)dx=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Theta_{i}(b_{i}+R_{i}(x))^{2}dx=0.

(4.2)

Since
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
and
\Theta_{1}
,
\Theta_{2}
are fixed constants, (
4.2
) implies that
b_{3}
is a function of
\Theta_{3}
and
R
, more precisely,

\displaystyle b_{3}

\displaystyle=b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\frac{1}{2\pi\Theta_{3}}\left(\Theta_{1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(b_{1}+R_{1}(x))^{2}dx+\Theta_{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(b_{2}+R_{2}(x))^{2}dx+\Theta_{3}\int_{0}^{2\pi}R_{3}(x)^{2}dx\right)}

(4.3)

If
\Theta_{3},b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)\neq 0
, then its derivative with respect to
R
is given by

\displaystyle Db_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)[h]

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{dt}b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R+th)\bigg|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\Theta_{3}b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)}\left(\Theta_{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}R_{1}(x)h_{1}(x)dx+\Theta_{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}R_{2}(x)h_{2}(x)dx+\Theta_{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}R_{3}(x)h_{3}(x)dx\right),

(4.4)

where we used
\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{i}(x)dx=0
for
h_{i}\in X^{k,m}
.

Note that for sufficiently small
\|R_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
|\Theta_{3}-\Theta^{*}_{3,m}|
, where
\Theta^{*}_{3,m}
is as defined in Lemma
4.24
, we can choose
b_{3}=b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R_{i})
so that (
4.2
) is compatible with
b_{2}>b_{3}>0
(see Lemma
4.24
). Therefore, a 4-tuple
(\Theta_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})=:(\Theta_{3},R)
uniquely determines
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
such that the boundary of the
i
th patch surrounds the
j
th patch if
i<j
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega dx=0
. In the proof,
\Theta_{3}
will play the role of the bifurcation parameter and we will look for a bifurcation from
(\Theta^{*}_{3,m},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times(X^{k,m})^{3}
.

With this setting, the system (
2.3
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle 0=G(\Theta_{3},R):=(G_{1}(\Theta_{3},R),G_{2}(\Theta_{3},R),G_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)),

(4.5)

where

\displaystyle G_{j}(\Theta_{3},R):=\mathcal{F}_{j}(b(\Theta_{3},R),\Theta_{3},R)),\text{ and }b(\Theta_{3},R):=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}(\Theta_{3},R)),\quad j=1,2,3.

(4.6)

Now, we are ready to state the main theorem of this section:

Theorem 4.1

Let
k\geq 3
and
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Theta_{1}=b_{1}=1
and
b_{2}
and
\Theta_{2}
as in (
4.1
). Then for some
s_{0}=s_{0}(m,k,b_{2},\Theta_{2})>0
, there exists a bifurcation curve
[0,s_{0})\ni s\mapsto(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))\in\mathbb{R}\times(X^{k,m})^{3}
such that for each
s\in(0,s_{0})
,
(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))
is a solution of the equation (
4.5
) and
(R(s))\neq 0\in(X^{k,m})^{3}
. The bifurcation curve emanates from
(\Theta_{3}(0),R(0))=(\Theta^{*}_{3,m},0)
, where
\Theta^{*}_{3,m}
is defined in Lemma
4.24
.

Theorem
4.1
immediately implies the existence of non-radial stationary vortex patches with finite kinetic energy.

Corollary 4.2

Let
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq 3
. Then there is an
m
-fold symmetric stationary patch solution of the 2D Euler equation with
H^{k}
-regular boundary and finite kinetic energy, that is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\nabla\left(\omega*\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\cdot|\right)\right|^{2}dx<\infty.

Proof.

By the definition of
b_{3}
in (
4.2
), each
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
which is determined by
(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))
for
s\in(0,s_{0})
, satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega dx=0
. This is equivalent to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\left(\omega*\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|\cdot|\right)|^{2}dx<\infty
, (see (
3.1
)).
∎

The existence of the bifurcation curves will be proved by means of a Nash-Moser iteration scheme (Theorem
4.7
). The proof of Theorem
4.1
will be accomplished in Subsection
4.5
by checking the hypotheses of Theorem
4.7
.

4.2
Compactly supported velocity

In this subsection, we digress briefly to observe an interesting consequence of Theorem
4.1
. Thanks to a simple maximum principle lemma, it can be shown that each stationary solution on the bifurcation curves has compactly supported velocity.

Lemma 4.3

(the key Lemma) Assume that
\omega\in L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
n\geq 2
is compactly supported and let
\Omega
be the unbounded connected component of
\text{supp}(\omega)^{c}
. We additionally assume that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\omega dx=0
. Then for
f:=\omega*\mathcal{N}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|&\text{ if }n=2\\
\frac{1}{n(2-n)V_{n}}|x|^{2-n},\quad V_{n}:=\frac{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2}+1)}&\text{ if }n>2,\end{cases}

it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega}f(x)=\max_{x\in\partial\Omega}f(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{x\in\Omega}f(x)=\min_{x\in\partial\Omega}f(x).

Consequently, if
f
is constant on
\partial\Omega
, then
f
is constant in
\Omega
.

Remark 4.4

The above lemma does not hold without the assumption
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\omega dx=0
. For example,
f:=1_{B_{1}}*\mathcal{N}
is harmonic in
\Omega:=B_{1}^{c}
, while
\max_{\Omega}f
is unbounded in
\Omega^{c}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the maximum part since we can apply the argument to
-f
for the minimum part. The proof is classical but we present a proof for the sake of completeness.

Let
M:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}f(x)
. Then
A:=\left\{x\in\Omega:f(x)=M\right\}
is relatively closed in
\Omega
, since
f
is continuous. Furthermore, since
f
is harmonic in
\Omega
, the mean value property yields that
A
is open. Therefore
A
must be either
\emptyset
or
\Omega
since
\Omega
is connected. If
A=\Omega
, then the result follows trivially. Now, let us suppose that
A=\emptyset
. Towards a contradiction, assume that
M>\max_{x\in\partial\Omega}f(x)
. Since
f
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(this property still holds when
n=2
, thanks to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega dx=0
), therefore the only possible case is that

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\to\infty}f(x)=M.

(4.7)

Let us consider
\phi(r):=\frac{1}{|\partial B_{r}|}\int_{|x|=r}f(x)d\sigma(x)
. For any sufficiently large
r
such that
\Omega^{c}\subset B_{r}
, where
B_{r}
is the ball centered at the origin with radius r, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\phi(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dr}\frac{1}{|S_{n-1}|}\int_{|x|=1}f(rx)d\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{|\partial B_{r}|}\int_{|x|<r}\omega(x)dx=0,

Furthermore, (
4.7
) yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(r)=M,

hence, we have
\phi(r)=M
for all sufficiently large
r
. For such
r>0
, we have

\displaystyle M=\frac{1}{|\partial B_{r}|}\int_{|x|=r}f(x)d\sigma\leq\max_{x\in\partial B_{r}}f(x)\leq M.

This implies
A
cannot be empty, which is a contradiction. Hence
M=\max_{x\in\partial\Omega}f(x)
.
∎

Corollary 4.5

Let
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq 3
. There exists an m-fold symmetric stationary patch solution of the 2D Euler equation with
H^{k}
-regular boundary and compactly supported velocity.

Proof.

Let
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
be the vorticity determined by
(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))
for
s\in(0,s_{0})
in Theorem
4.1
. From (
2.1
) and (
2.3
), it follows that its stream function
f:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\omega*\log|\cdot|
is constant on the outmost boundary
\partial D_{1}
. However, Lemma
4.3
implies that
\sup_{D_{1}^{c}}f=\inf_{D_{1}^{c}}f
, therefore,
f
is constant in
D_{1}^{c}
. This proves that
\text{supp}(\nabla^{\perp}f)\subset D_{1}
.
∎

Remark 4.6

In this paper we focus on patch type solutions
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Theta_{i}1_{D_{i}}
. Lemma
4.3
is still applicable for smooth
\omega
as well as long as the boundary of
\Omega:=\text{supp}(\omega)
can be approximated by regular level curves of
\omega
(See Figure
3
). This is due to the fact that the stream function of the smooth stationary
\omega
must be constant on each regular level set of
\omega
(see
[
51
, Section 1]
), hence the stream function has to be constant on each connected component of
\partial\Omega
. If
\partial\Omega
has only one connected component, then the velocity vanishes in the unbounded component of
\Omega^{c}
.

Figure 3:
Illustration for smooth stationary
\omega
where
\Omega:=\text{supp}(\omega)
is colored in blue, whose boundary is not necessarily connected. The dashed lines are regular level sets of
\omega
, which converges to the outermost boundary. In such case, the stream function is constant on the outermost boundary
\partial\Omega^{out}
, thus Lemma
4.3
implies that the velocity vanishes in the unbounded part of
\Omega^{c}
.

4.3
Nash-Moser theorem

We first prove a bifurcation theorem using the Nash-Moser scheme under some assumptions, which will turn out to be satisfied by our nonlinear functional. We follow the ideas from Berti
[
7
]
.

Let
2\leq m\in\mathbb{N}
fixed. We denote

X^{k}:=X^{k,m},\quad Y^{k}:=Y^{k,m},\quad C^{\infty}:=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}),\quad R:=(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\in\left(X^{k+1}\right)^{3}\cap(C^{\infty})^{3},\quad\partial_{\Theta}:=\partial_{\Theta_{3}},

for simplicity. Furthermore, for a Banach space
X
and an element
R\in X
, we denote the norm of
R
by
|R|_{X}:=\rVert R\rVert_{X}
. In addition, we use the notation
A\lesssim_{a,b}B
if there exists a constant
C=C(a,b)>0
depending on some variables
a,b
such that
A\leq CB
. We also use
c_{0},c_{1},....
to denote universal constants that may vary from line to line.

Theorem 4.7

Assume that there exists
\Theta_{3}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
and an open neighborhood
I\times V^{3}
of
(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times\left(X^{3}\right)^{3}
such that for each
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
,
G:I\times\left(X^{k+1}\right)^{3}\mapsto\left(Y^{k}\right)^{3}
satisfies the following: For
(\Theta_{3},R)\in I\times\left(V^{3}\cap(C^{\infty})^{3}\right)

(a)

(Existence of a curve of trivial solutions)
G(\Theta_{3},0)=0
for all
\Theta_{3}\in I
.

(b)

(Regularity) It holds that

\displaystyle|G(\Theta_{3},R)|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}1+|R|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}},

(4.8)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3},R)\right|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}1+\left|R\right|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}

(4.9)

\displaystyle|D^{2}G(\Theta_{3},R)[h,h]|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}(1+|R|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}})|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}^{2}

(4.10)

\displaystyle|\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3},R)[h]|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}(1+|R|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}})|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle|\partial_{\Theta}D^{2}G(\Theta^{*}_{3},R)[h,h]|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}(1+|R|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}})|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}^{2}

(4.12)

\displaystyle|\partial_{\Theta\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3},R)[h]|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}(1+|R|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}})|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}

(4.13)

(c)

(Decomposition of
DG
)
DG(\Theta_{3},R)\in\mathcal{L}((X^{k+1})^{3};(Y^{k})^{3})
has the following decomposition:

\displaystyle DG(\Theta_{3},R)[h]=a(\Theta_{3},R)[h]+A(\Theta_{3},R)[h],

such that
a(\Theta_{3},R)\in\mathcal{L}((X^{k+1})^{3},(Y^{k})^{3})
,
A(\Theta_{3},R)\in\mathcal{L}((X^{k+1})^{3},Y^{k+1}\times(Y^{k})^{2})
. Also, there exists
0<\eta<1
such that if
\rVert R\rVert_{(H^{k+2})^{3}}\leq\eta
, then

\displaystyle{|a(\Theta_{3},R)[h]|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}|G(\Theta_{3},R)|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}},

(4.14)

\displaystyle|A(\Theta_{3},R)[h]|_{Y^{k+1}\times(Y^{k})^{2}}\lesssim_{k}(1+|R|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}})|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}},

(4.15)

(
\tilde{c}
-1)

A:\mathbb{R}\times(X^{k+3})^{3}\mapsto\mathcal{L}((X^{k+1})^{3},Y^{k+1}\times(Y^{k})^{2})
is Lipschitz continuous. That is, if

(\Theta_{3}^{1},R^{1}),\ (\Theta_{3}^{2},R^{2})\in I\times(V^{3}\cap(C^{\infty})^{3}),

and
\rVert R^{1}\rVert_{(H^{k+3}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}},\rVert R^{2}\rVert_{(H^{k+3}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}}\leq 1
,
it holds that

\displaystyle|A(\Theta_{3}^{1},R^{1})[h]-A(\Theta_{3}^{2},R^{2})|_{(Y^{k})^{3}}\lesssim_{k}\left(|\Theta_{3}^{1}-\Theta_{3}^{2}|+|R^{1}-R^{2}|_{(X^{k+3})^{3}}\right)|h|_{(X^{k+1})^{3}}.

(4.16)

(
\tilde{c}
-2)

(Tame estimates) There exists
0<\eta<1
such that if
\rVert R\rVert_{(H^{k+3}(\mathbb{T}))^{3}}\leq\eta
, and
A(\Theta_{3},R)[h]=z
for some
z\in(C^{\infty})^{3}
and
h\in\text{Ker}(A(\Theta^{*}_{3},0))^{\perp}
, then
h\in(C^{\infty})^{3}
. Furthermore, for any even
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{0\right\}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|h\right|_{(X^{k+1+\sigma})^{3}}\lesssim_{k,\sigma}(1+\left|R\right|_{(X^{k+4+\sigma})^{3}})\left|z\right|_{Y^{k+1}\times(Y^{k})^{2}}+\left|z\right|_{Y^{k+1+\sigma}\times(Y^{k+\sigma})^{2}}.

(4.17)

(d)

(Fredholm index zero) There exist non-zero vectors
v
and
w
such that
v
and
w
are supported on the
m
-th Fourier mode and

\displaystyle\text{Ker}(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))=\text{span}\left\{v\right\},\quad\text{Im}(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))^{\perp}=\text{span}\left\{w\right\},

(e)

(Transversality)
\partial_{\Theta}A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)[v]\notin\text{Im}(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))
.

Then, for any
k_{0}\geq 2
, there exist a constant
s_{0}=s_{0}(k_{0})>0
and a curve
[0,s_{0})\ni s\mapsto(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))\in I\times(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
such that
G(\Theta_{3},R)=0
and
R(s)\neq 0
for
s>0
. The curve emanates from
(\Theta_{3}(0),R(0))=(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)
.

Remark 4.8

Note that the evenness of
\sigma
for the tame estimate in
(\tilde{c}-2)
is simply because
X^{k}
is a space of even functions and any odd order derivatives of
h\in X^{k}
are even.

The rest of this section is devoted to prove Theorem
4.7
.

Towards the proof, let
k_{0}\geq 2
be fixed. We define the projections
P:(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}\mapsto\text{Ker}(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))
and
Q:(Y^{k_{0}})^{3}\mapsto\text{Im}(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))^{\perp}
by

\displaystyle PR:=\left(v\cdot R\right)v\quad\text{ and }\quad QR:=(w\cdot R)w,

(4.18)

where
(f\cdot g)
denotes the usual
L^{2}
inner product. Note that from the assumptions
(d)
and
(e)
, we make an ansatz that for sufficiently small
s>0
, the bifurcation curve
s\mapsto(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))
can be written as

(\Theta_{3}(s),R(s))=\left(\Theta^{*}_{3}+v\cdot\tilde{R}(s),sv+(I-P)\tilde{R}(s)\right),

for some
\tilde{R}(s)\in(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
such that
|(I-P)\tilde{R}|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}=o(s)
. From this ansatz, we define a family of functionals
\tilde{G_{s}}:(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}\mapsto(Y^{k_{0}})^{3}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{s}(R):=G(\Theta^{*}_{3}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R),

(4.19)

and look for
R\in(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
such that
\tilde{G}_{s}(R)=0
and
|(I-P)R|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}=o(s)
for sufficiently small
s>0
. This will be achieved by Newton’s method, where the first approximate solution is
R=0
. To perform Newton’s method, we need to study the linearized operator
D\tilde{G}_{s}
at each approximate solution
R\neq 0
: For
h\in(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
, which can be directly computed from (
4.19
),

\displaystyle D\tilde{G}_{s}(R)[h]

\displaystyle:=D_{R}\tilde{G}_{s}(R)[h]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)(v\cdot h)+DG(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)h]

\displaystyle=:T_{s}(R)[h]+a(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)h],

(4.20)

where

\displaystyle T_{s}(R)[h]:=\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)(v\cdot h)+A(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)h].

(4.21)

The above decomposition of
D\tilde{G}_{s}
into
T_{s}+a
follows from the assumption
(c)
. Recall that Newton’s method relies on the “invertibility” of
T_{s}(R)
for small
s>0
. However, as we will see in the next lemma,
T_{s}
is not fully invertible between
(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
and
(Y^{k_{0}})^{3}
, because of its loss of derivatives. This is a motivation of adapting a Nash-Moser scheme in our proof. However, (
4.14
) suggests that the inverse of
T_{s}
is a good approximate right inverse of
D\tilde{G}_{s}
. In the next subsection, we will focus on the properties of
T_{s}(R)
.

4.3.1
Analysis of
{T}_{s}

We will look for a solution
R
to
\tilde{G}_{s}(R)=0
in
(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
for small
s>0
. In each step of Newton’s method (Nash-Moser iteration), we will regularize the approximate solution
R_{n}
(see (
4.58
)). The theorem will be achieved by proving that
R_{n}
converges in
(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
. However, we will also obtain boundedness of the sequence
R_{n}
in the higher norms, which is necessary because of the extra regularity conditions as in (c),
(\tilde{c}-1)
and
(\tilde{c}-2)
. For this reason, we will establish several lemmas assuming that an approximate solution
R
is more regular then
(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}
, which will turn out to be true at the end of the proof.

Lemma 4.9

Let
0<\epsilon<1
and
2\leq k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. There exist positive constants
s_{0}(\epsilon,k_{0}),\ c_{0}(\epsilon,k_{0})
and
0<\delta(\epsilon,k_{0})<1
such that for each
0<s<s_{0}
, the following holds: If

\displaystyle|PR|_{(X^{k_{0}+2})^{3}}\leq s^{\epsilon},

(4.22)

\displaystyle|(I-P)R|_{(X^{k_{0}+2})^{3}}\leq s^{1+\epsilon},

(4.23)

\displaystyle|R|_{(H^{k_{0}+4})^{3}}\leq\delta,

(4.24)

then

(A)

For all
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle(\Theta^{*}_{3}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)\in I\times V^{3},

(4.25)

where
V^{3}
is as in Theorem
4.7
. Therefore,

\displaystyle T_{s}(R)[h]:=\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)(v\cdot h)+A(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)h],

is well-defined.

(B)

T_{s}(R):(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}\mapsto Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}
is an isomorphism and

\displaystyle|T_{s}(R)[h]|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\leq c_{0}|h|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}},

(4.26)

\displaystyle|T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{s}|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}},

(4.27)

\displaystyle\left|\left(D\tilde{G}_{s}(R)\circ T_{s}(R)^{-1}-I\right)[z]\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}})^{3}}\leq c_{0}|\tilde{G}_{s}(R)|_{(Y^{k_{0}})^{3}}|T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}},

(4.28)

for some
c_{0}=c_{0}(\epsilon,k_{0})>0
.

If
R\in C^{\infty}
, then for any even
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{0\right\}
and
z\in X^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\times(X^{k_{0}+\sigma})^{2}
, we have
T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]\in\left(X^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\right)^{3}
. Also, we have that

\displaystyle\left|T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]\right|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\right)^{3}}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{s}\left((1+|R|_{(X^{k_{0}+4+\sigma})^{3}})|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}+|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\times(Y^{k_{0}+\sigma})^{2}}\right),

(4.29)

where
c_{0}
may depend on not only
\epsilon,k_{0}
but also
\sigma
.

(C)

Furthermore, we can choose
c_{0}
so that when
R=0
, the following hold:

\displaystyle|P(T_{s}(0)^{-1}[z])|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{s}|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}},

(4.30)

\displaystyle|(I-P)(T_{s}(0)^{-1}[z])|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c_{0}|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}.

(4.31)

We will frequently use

\displaystyle|T_{s}(R)^{-1}z|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+1}\right)^{3}}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{s}|z|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}},

(4.32)

which is more crude than (
4.27
).

Proof.

Let us fix
\epsilon>0
and
k_{0}\geq 2
. We will show that if (
4.22
)-(
4.24
) hold for sufficiently small
s>0
and for some small
\delta>0
depending on
\epsilon
, then (A),(B) and (C) hold for some
c_{0}>0
.

Proof of (A).
To see (A), notice that (
4.25
) is trivial. From the assumptions (b) and (c) in Theorem
4.7
, the linear operator
T_{s}(R)
is well-defined.

Proof of (B).
The estimate (
4.26
), follows from (
4.21
), (
4.15
), (
4.9
) and

|sv+(I-P)R|_{\left(H^{k_{0}+3}\right)^{3}}\lesssim 1+\delta\lesssim 1,

where the last inequality follows from (
4.24
). Before proving (
4.27
), we first prove that

\displaystyle|Q\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)|_{X^{k_{0}+1}\times(X^{k_{0}})^{2}}\geq cs,

(4.33)

for some
c=c(\epsilon)>0
. The above inequality is a consequence of the transversality condition
(e)
in Theorem
4.7
. In what follows,
c,c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
denote positive constants that may change from line to line and depend only on
\epsilon
but not on
s
.

By the regularity assumptions of
G
in (b) in Theorem
4.7
, we estimate the quantity in (
4.33
) using a Taylor expansion up to linear order: For a fixed
R
, we set
f(s,y):=Q\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)y)
, so that

\displaystyle f(s,R)=Q\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R).

(4.34)

Using the fundamental theorem of calculus, we can write

\displaystyle f(s,R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\left(f(s,tR)\right)dt+f(s,0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\left(f(s,tR)\right)dt+f(0,0)+\int_{0}^{s}\frac{d}{dt}f(t,0)dt.

In terms of
G
, (
4.34
) and the above equality give us that

\displaystyle|Q\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}=|f(s,R)|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq-{\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\|Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+t((I-P)R))\|_{\mathcal{L}((X^{k_{0}+2})^{3},(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3})}|(I-P)R|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+2}\right)^{3}}}

\displaystyle\ -{|Q\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,0)|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}

\displaystyle\ +{\bigg|\int_{0}^{s}Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,tv)[v]dt\bigg|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}

\displaystyle=:-L_{1}-L_{2}+L_{3},

(4.35)

where the first term after the inequality follows from b) in Theorem
4.7
and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{d}{dt}\left(f(s,tR)\right)\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left|\frac{d}{dt}\left(f(s,tR)\right)\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}dt

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\left|D_{R}f(s,tR)[R]\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}},

and the rest follows simply from the triangular inequality. Then the regularity assumption (b) in Theorem
4.7
yields that (recall
\rVert R\rVert_{(X^{k_{0}+4})^{3}}<\delta<1
,)

\displaystyle L_{1}\leq c_{1}|(I-P)R|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+2}\right)^{3}}\leq c_{1}s^{1+\epsilon},

(4.36)

where the last inequality follows from (
4.23
). For
L_{2}
, we use (a) in Theorem
4.7
to obtain

\displaystyle L_{2}=0.

(4.37)

To estimate
L_{3}
, we compute

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle\geq\bigg|\int_{0}^{s}Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3}^{*},tv)[v]dt\bigg|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}-\int_{0}^{s}\sup_{u\in(0,|v\cdot R|)}\bigg|Q\partial_{\Theta\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3}^{*}+u,tv)[v]\bigg|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}|v\cdot R|dt.

(4.38)

For the first integral, we can write

\displaystyle Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta^{*}_{3},tv)[v]

\displaystyle=Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)[v]+\int_{0}^{t}Q\partial_{\Theta}D^{2}G(\Theta^{*}_{3},uv)[v,v]du

\displaystyle=QA(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)[v]+\int_{0}^{t}Q\partial_{\Theta}D^{2}G(\Theta^{*}_{3},uv)[v,v]du,

where the last equality follows from (a) and (c) in Theorem
4.7
, which shows
a(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)=0
. For the integral term,
using (
4.12
), we have

\left|Q\partial_{\Theta}D^{2}G(\Theta^{*}_{3},uv)[v,v]\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\lesssim\left|Q\partial_{\Theta}D^{2}G(\Theta^{*}_{3},uv)[v,v]\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\lesssim(1+\rVert R\rVert_{(X^{k_{0}+4})^{3}})\rVert v\rVert_{(X^{k_{0}+2})^{3}}\leq c,

where the last inequality follows from (
4.24
). Therefore

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{s}Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta_{3}^{*},tv)[v]dt\bigg|_{X^{k_{0}+1}\times(X^{k_{0}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq s\left|QA(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)[v]\right|_{X^{k_{0}+1}\times(X^{k_{0}})^{2}}-\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{t}cdudt

\displaystyle\geq s\left|Q\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)[v]\right|_{X^{k_{0}+1}\times(X^{k_{0}})^{2}}-c_{2}s^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c_{3}s-c_{2}s^{2},

where the last inequality follows from the transversality (e). For the second integral in (
4.38
), we have that for sufficiently small
s>0
,

\sup_{u\in(0,|v\cdot R|),t\in(0,s)}\left|\partial_{\Theta\Theta}DG(\Theta^{*}_{3}+u,tv)[v]\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\lesssim\sup_{t\in(0,s)}(1+|tv|_{(X^{k_{0}+4})^{3}})|v|_{(X^{k_{0}+2})^{3}}\leq c_{1},

for sufficiently small
s
, which follows from (
4.13
).
Hence using (
4.22
), we obtain from (
4.38
) that

\displaystyle L_{3}\geq c_{3}s-c_{2}s^{2}-c_{1}s^{1+\epsilon}

(4.39)

Thus the claim (
4.33
) follows from (
4.3.1
) (
4.36
), (
4.37
) and (
4.39
) for small
s
, depending on
\epsilon
.

Towards the proof of (
4.27
), we will consider how to invert
A(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)]
. Since the dimension of
\text{Im}(DG(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)^{\perp})
is one and
w
is the basis of
\text{Im}(DG(\Theta_{3}^{*},0))^{\perp}
, the above claim (
4.33
) proves that

\displaystyle\left|w\cdot\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)\right|>cs.

(4.40)

To simplify the notations,
we denote

\displaystyle\tilde{A}[h]:=A(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)[(I-P)h],

(4.41)

\displaystyle\partial_{\Theta}G:=\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R).

We denote by
Q_{1}
the projection from
Y^{k_{0}+1}\times\left(Y^{k_{0}}\right)^{2}
into
\text{Im}(\tilde{A})^{\perp}
. Since the norm of
R
is small, we expect that the functional structure of
\tilde{A}
should be similar to the structure of
A(\Theta^{*}_{3},0)
. Indeed, by continuity hypotheses (
4.16
), (
4.24
) and Lemma
A.15
, where we can think of
\tilde{A}
as a linear map from
\text{Ker}(A(\Theta^{*}_{3},0))
, we can choose
\delta
small enough so that if (
4.24
) holds, then there exists
0\neq w_{1}\in Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\text{Im}(\tilde{A})^{\perp}=\text{span}\left\{w_{1}\right\}

(4.42)

\displaystyle|w-w_{1}|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\leq c\delta,

(4.43)

\displaystyle|\tilde{A}|_{\mathcal{L}(\text{Ker}\left(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)\right)^{\perp},\text{Im}(\tilde{A}))}\leq c,

(4.44)

\displaystyle|\tilde{A}^{-1}|_{\mathcal{L}(\text{Im}(\tilde{A}),\text{Ker}\left(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)\right)^{\perp})}\leq c.

(4.45)

Now, we claim that we can further restrict
\delta
if necessary so that

\displaystyle\left|w_{1}\cdot\partial_{\Theta}G\right|>cs.

(4.46)

In fact, note that (
4.22
)-(
4.23
) imply that
R\in(X^{k_{0}+2})^{3}
, thus by (
4.9
), we have
\partial_{\Theta}G\in(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}\subset Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}
. Also we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\Theta}G

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{t}\left(\partial_{\Theta}G(\Theta^{*}_{3}+v\cdot R,t(sv+(I-P)R))\right)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\Theta}DG(\Theta^{*}_{3}+v\cdot R,t(sv+(I-P)R))[sv+(I-P)R]dt,

thus

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\leq\left|\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c|sv+(I-P)R|_{(X^{k_{0}+2})^{2}}\leq cs,

(4.47)

where the second inequality follows from (
4.9
) and the last inequality follows from (
4.23
). Then it follows that (recall that
f\cdot g
is the dot product in
L^{2}
space),

\displaystyle|\partial_{\Theta}G\cdot w_{1}|

\displaystyle\geq|\partial_{\Theta}G\cdot w|-|\partial_{\Theta}G\cdot(w_{1}-w)|

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}s-|\partial_{\Theta}G\cdot(w_{1}-w)|

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}s-cs|w-w_{1}|_{X^{k_{0}+1}\times(X^{k_{0}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq c_{1}s-c\delta s,

where we used (
4.40
), (
4.47
) and (
4.43
) for the second, third and the fourth inequality, respectively. Hence, assuming
\delta
is small, we have (
4.46
).

To prove (
4.27
), pick an arbitrary
z\in Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}
. There exists a unique
\eta\in\text{Ker}\left(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)\right)
and a unique
h\in\text{Ker}\left(A(\Theta_{3}^{*},0)\right)^{\perp}
such that

\displaystyle(v\cdot\eta)Q_{1}\partial_{\Theta}G=Q_{1}z

(4.48)

\displaystyle(v\cdot\eta)(I-Q_{1})\partial_{\Theta}G+\tilde{A}[h]=(I-Q_{1})z.

(4.49)

In fact, there exists a unique
\eta
in (
4.48
) thanks to (
4.46
) and that
Q_{1}
is the projection to a one-dimensional space spanned by
w_{1}
. Once
\eta
is fixed, the existence and the uniqueness of
h
in (
4.49
) follows from (
4.45
).
Once
\eta
and
h
are determined, it is clear that

\displaystyle T_{s}(R)[h+\eta]=(v\cdot(h+\eta))\partial_{\Theta}G+\tilde{A}[\eta+h]=(v\cdot\eta)\partial_{\Theta}G+\tilde{A}[h]=z,

(4.50)

where we used
v\cdot h=0
and
\tilde{A}[\eta]=\tilde{A}[(I-P)\eta]=0
, which follows from the definition of
\tilde{A}
in (
4.41
).
Therefore
T(R)^{-1}z=h+\eta
. Furthermore we have from (
4.48
) and (
4.49
) that

\displaystyle|\eta|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c\frac{|z\cdot w_{1}|}{|\partial_{\Theta}G\cdot w_{1}|}

(4.51)

\displaystyle|h|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq|\tilde{A}^{-1}(I-Q_{1})\partial_{\Theta}G(v\cdot\eta)|_{{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}}+|\tilde{A}^{-1}(I-Q_{1})z|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}.

(4.52)

Using (
4.46
) and (
4.45
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\eta|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c\frac{|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}{s},

(4.53)

\displaystyle|h|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c|\partial_{\Theta}G|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\frac{|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}{s}+c|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\leq c\frac{|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}{s},

(4.54)

where we used (
4.47
). This proves (
4.27
).

To show (
4.28
), we compute

\displaystyle\left(D\tilde{G}_{s}(R)\circ T_{s}(R)^{-1}-I\right)[z]=a(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)\circ T(R)^{-1}[z],

which follows from (
4.20
). This implies

\displaystyle|\left(D\tilde{G}_{s}(R)\circ T_{s}(R)^{-1}-I\right)[z]|_{\left(X^{k_{0}}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq c|a(\Theta_{3}^{*}-v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)\circ T(R)^{-1}[z]|_{\left(X^{k_{0}}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq c|G(\Theta_{3}^{*}+v\cdot R,sv+(I-P)R)|_{\left(Y^{k_{0}}\right)^{3}}|T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+1}\right)^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq c|\tilde{G}_{s}(R)|_{\left(Y^{k_{0}}\right)^{3}}|T_{s}(R)^{-1}[z]|_{\left(X^{k_{0}+1}\right)^{3}},

where we used (
4.14
) to get the second inequality and used the definition of
\tilde{G}_{s}(R)
to obtain the last inequality.

In order to prove (
4.29
), we improve on the estimates in (
4.51
) and (
4.52
). For (
4.51
), recall that
\eta
is in a one-dimensional space, span
\left\{v\right\}
and
v
is supported on the
m
-th Fourier mode. Thus,

\displaystyle|\eta|_{(X^{k_{0}+1+\sigma})^{3}}\leq c|\eta|_{(X^{k_{0}+1})^{3}}\leq c\frac{|z|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}{s},

(4.55)

where
c
may depend on
\sigma
.
Furthermore,
(\tilde{c}
-2) in Theorem
4.7
and (
4.49
) give us that

\displaystyle|h|_{(X^{k_{0}+1+\sigma})^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq c(1+\left|R\right|_{(X^{k_{0}+4+\sigma})^{3}})\left(\left|(v\cdot\eta)\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}+\left|z\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\ +c\left(\left|(v\cdot\eta)\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\times(Y^{k_{0}+\sigma})^{2}}+\left|z\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\times(Y^{k_{0}+\sigma})^{2}}\right).

(4.56)

Using (
4.47
) and (
4.53
), we have

\displaystyle\left|(v\cdot\eta)\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}\leq\left|\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}}|v\cdot\eta|\leq c\left|z\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}.

For the higher norm of
\partial_{\Theta}G
, we use (
4.9
) and and (
4.53
) and obtain

\displaystyle\left|(v\cdot\eta)\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1+\sigma}\times(Y^{k_{0}+\sigma})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left|\partial_{\Theta}G\right|_{(Y^{k_{0}+1+\sigma})^{3}}|v\cdot\eta|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{s}\left(1+\left|R\right|_{(X^{k_{0}+2+\sigma})^{3}}\right)\left|z\right|_{Y^{k_{0}+1}\times(Y^{k_{0}})^{2}}.

Therefore, (
4.56
) gives us
